### LLM-Generated Feedback Supports Learning If Learners Choose to Use It
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17006v1)
- **Authors**: Danielle R. Thomas, Conrad Borchers, Shambhavi Bhushan, Erin Gatz, Shivang Gupta, Kenneth R. Koedinger
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate feedback, yet their impact on learning remains underexplored, especially compared to existing feedback methods. This study investigates how on-demand LLM-generated explanatory feedback influences learning in seven scenario-based tutor training lessons. Analyzing over 2,600 lesson completions from 885 tutor learners, we compare posttest performance among learners across three groups: learners who received feedback generated by gpt-3.5-turbo, those who declined it, and those without access. All groups received non-LLM corrective feedback. To address potential selection bias-where higher-performing learners may be more inclined to use LLM feedback-we applied propensity scoring. Learners with a higher predicted likelihood of engaging with LLM feedback scored significantly higher at posttest than those with lower propensity. After adjusting for this effect, two out of seven lessons showed statistically significant learning benefits from LLM feedback with standardized effect sizes of 0.28 and 0.33. These moderate effects suggest that the effectiveness of LLM feedback depends on the learners' tendency to seek support. Importantly, LLM feedback did not significantly increase completion time, and learners overwhelmingly rated it as helpful. These findings highlight LLM feedback's potential as a low-cost and scalable way to improve learning on open-ended tasks, particularly in existing systems already providing feedback without LLMs. This work contributes open datasets, LLM prompts, and rubrics to support reproducibility.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of on-demand, LLM-generated explanatory feedback in seven scenario-based tutor training lessons. It compares posttest performance of learners who received LLM feedback (using GPT-3.5-turbo), those who declined it, and a control group receiving only non-LLM corrective feedback. The study addresses selection bias through propensity scoring, analyzing over 2,600 lesson completions from 885 college students. The results show that learners with a higher predicted likelihood of using LLM feedback performed significantly better on the posttest. While the learning benefits from LLM feedback were statistically significant in some lessons, they were moderate in size. Importantly, LLM feedback did not increase completion time, and learners overwhelmingly found it helpful.  The study provides open datasets, LLM prompts, and rubrics to facilitate reproducibility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The study's novelty lies in its focus on scenario-based *tutor* training rather than student learning, a relatively less-explored area in the context of LLM feedback. While prior work has examined LLM-generated feedback in educational settings, the specific application to tutor training and the use of propensity scoring to mitigate selection bias provide a unique contribution. However, the overall approach is fairly incremental building upon prior work exploring the use of LLMs for feedback and the evaluation of feedback effectiveness.

* **Significance:** The findings are significant for several reasons. First, they suggest that LLMs can be a low-cost, scalable way to improve learning in open-ended tasks, particularly when integrated into existing systems already providing feedback. Second, they highlight the importance of learner engagement with the feedback; the effectiveness of LLM feedback is contingent on learners actively seeking and using it. Third, the provision of open datasets, prompts, and rubrics promotes reproducibility and encourages further research. However, the moderate effect sizes (0.28 and 0.33) temper the enthusiasm. The fact that learners had to *opt-in* to the LLM-provided feedback suggests a possible self-selection bias where those more dedicated may have been more prone to use the feedback, which could inflate the effect size. Furthermore, the small gains relative to the sophistication of the intervention mean that it may not be worth it for resource-constrained settings.

* **Strengths:**
    * Rigorous methodology, including propensity scoring to address selection bias.
    * Large sample size (885 participants, 2,648 lesson completions).
    * Open datasets and materials for reproducibility.
    * Focus on tutor training, a less-explored application of LLM feedback.
    * Measurement of both learning outcomes and learner perceptions.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * Moderate effect sizes. The actual magnitude of improvement seems fairly low, even if statistically significant.
    * Potential for residual selection bias, even after propensity score adjustment. While propensity scoring is employed, it is not a perfect solution and there may be unobserved confounders.
    * Lack of a "mandatory LLM feedback" condition. It is possible that requiring LLM feedback could have revealed a bigger effect.
    * Dependence on GPT-3.5-turbo, which is constantly evolving. The results may not generalize to other LLMs or future versions.

* **Potential Influence:** The study provides practical guidance for educators and instructional designers looking to incorporate LLMs into tutor training programs. It also underscores the importance of considering learner engagement and designing systems that encourage proactive help-seeking. The open datasets and materials will facilitate future research in this area.
Score: 6.5
Rationale: The paper contributes novel insights into the application of LLM feedback in tutor training and uses robust statistical methods. However, the moderate effect sizes and limitations related to selection bias temper the enthusiasm for the results. The open resources are a valuable contribution. Therefore, a score of 6.5 is warranted, signifying a good but not exceptional contribution with clear room for improvement and further investigation.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### The Hidden Cost of an Image: Quantifying the Energy Consumption of AI Image Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17016v1)
- **Authors**: Giulia Bertazzini, Chiara Albisani, Daniele Baracchi, Dasara Shullani, Roberto Verdecchia
- **Abstract**: With the growing adoption of AI image generation, in conjunction with the ever-increasing environmental resources demanded by AI, we are urged to answer a fundamental question: What is the environmental impact hidden behind each image we generate? In this research, we present a comprehensive empirical experiment designed to assess the energy consumption of AI image generation. Our experiment compares 17 state-of-the-art image generation models by considering multiple factors that could affect their energy consumption, such as model quantization, image resolution, and prompt length. Additionally, we consider established image quality metrics to study potential trade-offs between energy consumption and generated image quality. Results show that image generation models vary drastically in terms of the energy they consume, with up to a 46x difference. Image resolution affects energy consumption inconsistently, ranging from a 1.3x to 4.7x increase when doubling resolution. U-Net-based models tend to consume less than Transformer-based one. Model quantization instead results to deteriorate the energy efficiency of most models, while prompt length and content have no statistically significant impact. Improving image quality does not always come at the cost of a higher energy consumption, with some of the models producing the highest quality images also being among the most energy efficient ones.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "The Hidden Cost of an Image: Quantifying the Energy Consumption of AI Image Generation" presents an empirical study investigating the energy consumption of various AI image generation models. It compares 17 state-of-the-art diffusion models, considering factors like model quantization, image resolution, and prompt length. The authors also evaluate the image quality using established metrics to explore trade-offs between energy consumption and quality. The study reveals significant variations in energy consumption across models (up to 46x difference), inconsistent effects of image resolution, a tendency for U-Net-based models to be more efficient than Transformer-based ones, and a counterintuitive increase in energy consumption with model quantization. The paper concludes that higher image quality doesn't necessarily correlate with higher energy consumption, with some efficient models producing high-quality results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Empirical Rigor:** The paper's primary strength is its thorough empirical approach. The extensive experiments (over 9000 runs) provide a valuable dataset for understanding the energy consumption of image generation models.
    *   **Comprehensive Scope:** Considering multiple influencing factors (quantization, resolution, prompt length) provides a more holistic view than studies focusing on a single factor.
    *   **Practical Relevance:**  Addressing the environmental impact of AI image generation is increasingly important given the growing adoption of these technologies and the associated energy demands.  The findings have practical implications for model selection and optimization.
    *   **Open Science Practices**: Making supplementary materials available aligns with open science principles.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty in Findings**: Some of the results are somewhat expected (e.g., higher resolution generally increasing energy consumption). However, the *quantification* of these effects across various models is valuable.
    *   **Lack of Explanatory Depth**: While the paper quantifies energy consumption, it doesn't deeply investigate *why* certain models are more efficient than others at an architectural level.  It notes the U-Net vs. Transformer difference but doesn't delve into the underlying reasons.
    *   **Metric Limitations:** Relying on FID and CLIPScore alone can be limiting as they don't fully capture all aspects of image quality (e.g., aesthetic appeal, specific task suitability). Furthermore they use ImageNet-1k, which had limited overlap with their prompts and necessitated some convoluted workarounds.
    *   **Generalizability Concerns:**  The study is performed on a single workstation. While consistent, the results may not fully generalize to different hardware configurations or cloud environments.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

The *primary* novelty lies in the *direct, comparative, empirical quantification of energy consumption across a wide range of popular generative models*. While individual studies have examined aspects of efficiency or image quality, this paper brings these aspects together within a unified experimental framework, specifically focusing on the intersection of energy usage, architectural choice, and image generation. It provides valuable data where, previously, only anecdotal information or extrapolations were available.

The counterintuitive finding that quantization *increases* energy use in *most* cases is particularly significant and challenges common assumptions about model optimization. This insight is likely to spur further research into more energy-aware quantization techniques. The findings regarding U-Net architectures being more energy-efficient than Transformer-based ones has value and can impact future choices. The null hypothesis result of prompt length provides useful information that it is not a major component in energy consumption.

*   **Potential Influence:**

The paper could influence:

    *   **Model Selection:**  Practitioners may use the results to choose more energy-efficient models for image generation tasks.
    *   **Future Research:**  The findings, particularly regarding quantization and architectural efficiency, could stimulate further research into energy-aware optimization methods.
    *   **Policy and Awareness:** The study contributes to the growing awareness of the environmental impact of AI, potentially influencing the development of policies and guidelines for sustainable AI development.

**Rigorous Rationale**

While providing valuable empirical data and challenging some preconceptions, the findings are not totally groundbreaking. Also, the paper somewhat lacks in-depth analysis and generalizability.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LSCD: Lomb-Scargle Conditioned Diffusion for Time series Imputation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17039v1)
- **Authors**: Elizabeth Fons, Alejandro Sztrajman, Yousef El-Laham, Luciana Ferrer, Svitlana Vyetrenko, Manuela Veloso
- **Abstract**: Time series with missing or irregularly sampled data are a persistent challenge in machine learning. Many methods operate on the frequency-domain, relying on the Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) which assumes uniform sampling, therefore requiring prior interpolation that can distort the spectra. To address this limitation, we introduce a differentiable Lomb--Scargle layer that enables a reliable computation of the power spectrum of irregularly sampled data. We integrate this layer into a novel score-based diffusion model (LSCD) for time series imputation conditioned on the entire signal spectrum. Experiments on synthetic and real-world benchmarks demonstrate that our method recovers missing data more accurately than purely time-domain baselines, while simultaneously producing consistent frequency estimates. Crucially, our method can be easily integrated into learning frameworks, enabling broader adoption of spectral guidance in machine learning approaches involving incomplete or irregular data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LSCD (Lomb–Scargle Conditioned Diffusion), a novel approach for time series imputation that integrates a differentiable Lomb-Scargle layer into a score-based diffusion model. This allows the model to directly condition on the spectral information of irregularly sampled or incomplete time series data, sidestepping the traditional FFT requirement for uniformly sampled data.  LSCD leverages the Lomb-Scargle periodogram, which can estimate power spectra without prior interpolation, mitigating spectral distortion.  The method is evaluated on synthetic and real-world datasets, demonstrating improved imputation accuracy and more consistent frequency estimates compared to time-domain baselines and approaches relying on FFT and pre-processing. The paper highlights the potential for broader adoption of spectral guidance in machine learning through its differentiable Lomb-Scargle implementation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *combination* of several existing techniques in a new and potentially beneficial way. Score-based diffusion models for time series imputation exist, and Lomb-Scargle periodograms are well-established. The key contribution is the integration of a *differentiable* Lomb-Scargle periodogram as a conditioning mechanism within a diffusion model framework. This differentiable nature and specific application to diffusion models is an important factor because it's rarely done.
*   **Significance:**  The significance stems from addressing a practical limitation of many time series analysis methods: the requirement for uniformly sampled data. The Lomb-Scargle periodogram's ability to handle irregular sampling directly is valuable.  The authors convincingly demonstrate that conditioning on the Lomb-Scargle spectrum can improve imputation performance and preserve frequency information, particularly under high missingness rates. Also, the effort of producing a differentiable implementation of the algorithm is a good step.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a Real Problem:** Irregularly sampled time series are common, and the paper provides a way to leverage spectral information without introducing distortion.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results, particularly on synthetic data with controlled missingness, are compelling. The ablation studies help isolate the contribution of different components. The real-world datasets provide support for the method's practical utility.
    *   **Differentiable Lomb-Scargle:** Providing a differentiable implementation makes this technique more readily usable within modern deep learning frameworks.
    *   **Improved Frequency Estimation:** The paper shows improvements in frequency estimation (S-MAE and LFE), addressing a key concern with data imputation: whether the imputed data retains the original signal characteristics.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** The reliance on diffusion models can be computationally expensive, both during training and inference. Although the paper mentions computation time, it might be useful to compare the computational complexity more rigorously.
    *   **Reliance on Sinusoidal Assumption (Lomb-Scargle limitation):** The periodogram relies on the underlying data being sinusoidal in shape. The authors note this in Appendix B, and mention it could bias the training. This is an important factor to consider.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to more advanced continuous approaches:** While the paper compares against some baselines, it lacks a comparison against SOTA methods tailored for irregular data like NCDSSM or MADS.
    *   **Limited real-world data:** The datasets and baselines in the experimentation need to be improved in the following aspects: i) adding more real-world datasets with varying characteristics and types of missingness, ii) including state-of-the-art methods for time-series imputation and irregular time-series imputation, and iii) providing a complexity analysis comparing different methods.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence time series analysis by promoting the use of spectral information in imputation, particularly when dealing with irregular or missing data. It could spur further research into differentiable spectral methods and their applications in machine learning.
*   **Caveats:** The experimental evaluation could be made more comprehensive. The computational cost should be compared more thoroughly.

**Rigorous Rationale for Assigned Score:**

The paper presents a novel *integration* of existing techniques to address a relevant and practical problem in time series analysis. The differentiable Lomb-Scargle periodogram implementation is a valuable contribution, and the empirical results are convincing. However, the sinusoidal limitation of the Lomb-Scargle periodogram *is a key consideration,* that, when combined with the potentially high computational cost, holds the paper back from being a genuinely high-impact work. Finally, a limited comparison with SOTA irregular data and complex time series challenges the evaluation. Therefore, considering the balance of novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, a score of 6 seems reasonable.
<br>
Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### MUCAR: Benchmarking Multilingual Cross-Modal Ambiguity Resolution for Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17046v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaolong Wang, Zhaolu Kang, Wangyuxuan Zhai, Xinyue Lou, Yunghwei Lai, Ziyue Wang, Yawen Wang, Kaiyu Huang, Yile Wang, Peng Li, Yang Liu
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated significant advances across numerous vision-language tasks. Due to their strong image-text alignment capability, MLLMs can effectively understand image-text pairs with clear meanings. However, effectively resolving the inherent ambiguities in natural language and visual contexts remains challenging. Existing multimodal benchmarks typically overlook linguistic and visual ambiguities, relying mainly on unimodal context for disambiguation and thus failing to exploit the mutual clarification potential between modalities. To bridge this gap, we introduce MUCAR, a novel and challenging benchmark designed explicitly for evaluating multimodal ambiguity resolution across multilingual and cross-modal scenarios. MUCAR includes: (1) a multilingual dataset where ambiguous textual expressions are uniquely resolved by corresponding visual contexts, and (2) a dual-ambiguity dataset that systematically pairs ambiguous images with ambiguous textual contexts, with each combination carefully constructed to yield a single, clear interpretation through mutual disambiguation. Extensive evaluations involving 19 state-of-the-art multimodal models--encompassing both open-source and proprietary architectures--reveal substantial gaps compared to human-level performance, highlighting the need for future research into more sophisticated cross-modal ambiguity comprehension methods, further pushing the boundaries of multimodal reasoning.
- **Summary**: The paper "MUCAR: Benchmarking Multilingual Cross-Modal Ambiguity Resolution for Multimodal Large Language Models" introduces a new benchmark designed to evaluate the ability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to resolve ambiguities across multilingual and cross-modal scenarios. MUCAR includes a multilingual dataset where textual ambiguities are resolved by visual contexts, and a dual-ambiguity dataset pairing ambiguous images with ambiguous text. The authors evaluated 19 state-of-the-art MLLMs and found substantial gaps compared to human-level performance. They also propose a simple agent-based framework to improve ambiguity resolution.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the explicit focus on *cross-modal ambiguity resolution* in a *multilingual* setting. While previous benchmarks have explored unimodal ambiguities or multimodality in general, the combined emphasis of both is a definite step forward. The construction of the "dual-ambiguity" dataset where both visual and textual modalities are ambiguous yet resolvable together is also a valuable contribution, highlighting the potential for mutual disambiguation.
    *   **Comprehensive Dataset:** The MUCAR dataset is significant in size (1278 instances), spans three languages (Chinese, English, Malay), and includes a thorough categorization of ambiguity types (Polysemy, Homonymy, Grammar, Semantics, Specialized, Cultural, and Dual-Ambiguity).
    *   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper includes extensive evaluations across a range of state-of-the-art MLLMs (both open-source and proprietary) and proposes metrics that are designed to capture nuances related to ambiguity resolution. The inclusion of human evaluation, although likely limited in scale (compared to automated metrics), enhances the reliability of the results. The agent-based framework, while simple, provides a starting point for research into better disambiguation strategies.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Linguistic Coverage:** The restriction to three languages is a significant limitation, making it harder to extrapolate findings to low-resource languages or languages with drastically different typologies. The generalizability of the findings is somewhat questionable given this restriction.
    *   **Simplified Scenarios:** Despite aiming for complex multimodal ambiguity, the scenarios might still be somewhat artificial and structured compared to the ambiguities encountered in genuinely unconstrained real-world data. The reliance on template-based output formats, while facilitating automatic evaluation, limits the models' ability to express more nuanced or free-form reasoning.
    *   **Agent-Based Framework:** While the introduction of the agent-based framework is a nice addition, its simplicity limits its impact. The details of the prompt engineering needed for that model could also help for replicability.
    *   **Dataset Size per Ambiguity Type:** It's also unclear from the paper how balanced the data is for each ambiguity type. Some categories might be severely underrepresented, influencing the evaluation results. The heavy focus in some instances on "grammar" is more syntax.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it highlights the current limitations of MLLMs in handling complex ambiguities, which are prevalent in real-world communication. MUCAR offers a targeted benchmark for evaluating and improving these capabilities. The benchmark can push the field to consider cross-modal interactions in greater depth and develop more sophisticated models capable of reasoning about context and semantics more accurately.

**Overall:** MUCAR represents a valuable contribution to the field by explicitly tackling the challenging problem of cross-modal and multilingual ambiguity resolution. While it has limitations in linguistic coverage and scenario complexity, it sets a clear direction for future research and provides a crucial benchmark for assessing MLLM performance in this critical area. The paper's thorough evaluation and novel dataset construction justify a solid score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Relaxed syntax modeling in Transformers for future-proof license plate recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17051v1)
- **Authors**: Florent Meyer, Laurent Guichard, Denis Coquenet, Guillaume Gravier, Yann Soullard, Bertrand Coüasnon
- **Abstract**: Effective license plate recognition systems are required to be resilient to constant change, as new license plates are released into traffic daily. While Transformer-based networks excel in their recognition at first sight, we observe significant performance drop over time which proves them unsuitable for tense production environments. Indeed, such systems obtain state-of-the-art results on plates whose syntax is seen during training. Yet, we show they perform similarly to random guessing on future plates where legible characters are wrongly recognized due to a shift in their syntax. After highlighting the flows of positional and contextual information in Transformer encoder-decoders, we identify several causes for their over-reliance on past syntax. Following, we devise architectural cut-offs and replacements which we integrate into SaLT, an attempt at a Syntax-Less Transformer for syntax-agnostic modeling of license plate representations. Experiments on both real and synthetic datasets show that our approach reaches top accuracy on past syntax and most importantly nearly maintains performance on future license plates. We further demonstrate the robustness of our architecture enhancements by way of various ablations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of "syntax shift" in license plate recognition (LPR) systems, where changes in license plate formats over time lead to performance degradation in Transformer-based models. The authors argue that Transformers over-rely on the syntax seen during training, resulting in poor generalization to new formats. They analyze positional and contextual information flows in Transformer encoder-decoders and identify sources of syntax bias. They propose SaLT (Syntax-Less Transformer), an architecture with a convolutional encoder and modifications to the decoder, designed to be less sensitive to training syntax. Experiments on real and synthetic datasets show that SaLT maintains accuracy on both old and new license plate formats, demonstrating its robustness. Ablation studies validate the effectiveness of the proposed architectural changes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main novelty lies in its explicit focus on and analysis of the "syntax shift" problem in LPR, particularly within the context of Transformer-based models. While previous works have tackled domain adaptation and robustness in LPR, this paper specifically isolates and attempts to mitigate the problem of models overly memorizing training syntax, which is a relevant challenge for real-world deployment. The proposed architecture modifications, while not entirely groundbreaking in isolation (e.g., using convolutional encoders), are combined strategically and justified with a clear understanding of the information flows and biases within the Transformer. It provides a clear, well-reasoned, and actionable solution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its practical implications. LPR systems require continuous operation and must adapt to changing license plate formats without constant retraining. The ability to maintain high accuracy across syntax changes is critical. The results demonstrate that SaLT can significantly improve generalization, making it a valuable contribution to building more robust LPR systems. The authors also present "LPR-MNIST," a novel dataset specifically designed for examining syntax shifts. This dataset is open-source, which encourages further research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Formulation:**  The paper provides a clear and well-defined formulation of the syntax shift problem in the context of LPR and Transformer models.
    *   **Analysis:** The authors perform a thorough analysis of the sources of syntax bias in Transformers, focusing on positional and contextual information flows. This is a crucial element for understanding the problem and designing a proper solution.
    *   **Solution Design:**  The design of SaLT is well-motivated, addressing the identified sources of bias with targeted architectural modifications.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The experiments are comprehensive, including evaluations on real and synthetic datasets, ablation studies, and comparisons with baseline models.
    *   **LPR-MNIST Dataset:** Creating and open-sourcing LPR-MNIST contributes to the community and facilitates further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Contributions:** Some of the architectural components (e.g., convolutional encoder) are not entirely new concepts. The value of this work lies in how these concepts have been adapted and combined with other components to tackle the given problem.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are primarily focused on French license plates. While the general principles should apply to other regions, further validation with diverse datasets would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Limited baseline:** The work only compares against TrOCR, it would be beneficial to compare against other state-of-the-art LPR approaches to emphasize the proposed SaLT advantages.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and adaptable LPR systems. The insights into syntax bias and the proposed debiasing techniques could be applied to other sequence recognition tasks where input distributions change over time. The LPR-MNIST dataset could also serve as a valuable benchmark for evaluating the robustness of future LPR models.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7**. The paper addresses a practical and relevant problem with a well-motivated solution. The analysis of syntax bias in Transformers is insightful, and the experimental results are convincing. However, the incremental nature of the architectural contributions and the limited scope of the experiments (primarily French license plates, limited baseline) prevent it from achieving a higher score. I believe this paper will become a relevant reference point when it comes to addressing syntax shift problems in transformer-based LPR models.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Concepts to Components: Concept-Agnostic Attention Module Discovery in Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17052v1)
- **Authors**: Jingtong Su, Julia Kempe, Karen Ullrich
- **Abstract**: Transformers have achieved state-of-the-art performance across language and vision tasks. This success drives the imperative to interpret their internal mechanisms with the dual goals of enhancing performance and improving behavioral control. Attribution methods help advance interpretability by assigning model outputs associated with a target concept to specific model components. Current attribution research primarily studies multi-layer perceptron neurons and addresses relatively simple concepts such as factual associations (e.g., Paris is located in France). This focus tends to overlook the impact of the attention mechanism and lacks a unified approach for analyzing more complex concepts. To fill these gaps, we introduce Scalable Attention Module Discovery (SAMD), a concept-agnostic method for mapping arbitrary, complex concepts to specific attention heads of general transformer models. We accomplish this by representing each concept as a vector, calculating its cosine similarity with each attention head, and selecting the TopK-scoring heads to construct the concept-associated attention module. We then propose Scalar Attention Module Intervention (SAMI), a simple strategy to diminish or amplify the effects of a concept by adjusting the attention module using only a single scalar parameter. Empirically, we demonstrate SAMD on concepts of varying complexity, and visualize the locations of their corresponding modules. Our results demonstrate that module locations remain stable before and after LLM post-training, and confirm prior work on the mechanics of LLM multilingualism. Through SAMI, we facilitate jailbreaking on HarmBench (+72.7%) by diminishing "safety" and improve performance on the GSM8K benchmark (+1.6%) by amplifying "reasoning". Lastly, we highlight the domain-agnostic nature of our approach by suppressing the image classification accuracy of vision transformers on ImageNet.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Scalable Attention Module Discovery (SAMD) and Scalar Attention Module Intervention (SAMI), a novel pipeline for interpreting and manipulating transformer models. SAMD is a concept-agnostic method that identifies attention heads associated with a given concept by calculating cosine similarity between a vector representation of the concept and the outputs of individual attention heads. SAMI is a simple intervention strategy that diminishes or amplifies the effect of a concept by scaling the output of the discovered attention module. The authors demonstrate SAMD and SAMI on diverse tasks, including analyzing learned concepts in language models, improving jailbreaking and reasoning capabilities, and controlling image classification in vision transformers.  The key benefit is it requires very sparse intervention, only acting on the identified module rather than the full set of parameters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a well-motivated and relatively novel approach to attention head analysis and intervention. While attribution methods are not entirely new, the concept-agnostic and scalable nature of SAMD, combined with the direct intervention strategy of SAMI, distinguishes it from prior work. SAMD specifically address the gap that previous attribution research primarily focused on multi-layer perceptron (MLP) neurons and addressed relatively simple concepts such as factual associations.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact of this work is substantial. Being able to identify and manipulate specific attention modules opens new avenues for understanding and controlling model behavior. The results demonstrating improved jailbreaking performance and reasoning on benchmarks are promising. The domain-agnostic nature of the approach, as shown by the vision transformer experiments, further enhances its significance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Concept-Agnostic:** The ability to analyze arbitrary concepts is a significant advantage over previous methods.
    *   **Scalability:** The method is designed to scale to large transformer models.
    *   **Simplicity:** SAMI is a simple and efficient intervention strategy.
    *   **Demonstrated Applications:** The paper provides compelling empirical results across different tasks and modalities.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides comprehensive experiments across a wide range of arbitrary concepts, models, and modalities in both language and vision. Through SAMD, we demonstrate that knowledge is sparsely encoded in the structure of large models, as indicated by the modules we uncover in all transformers and concepts analyzed. Using SAMI, we provide both qualitative and quantitative results to illustrate the effects of concept diminishment and amplification.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Causality:** The paper admits that it does not emphasize causality between the attention module and the targeted concept. While correlation is demonstrated, proving that manipulating the module *causes* the desired change in behavior would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The choice of *K* (number of attention heads in the module) and the scaling factor *s* in SAMI might require careful tuning and experimentation, which could limit its practical applicability.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in transformer interpretability and control.  It provides a new tool for researchers to explore the internal mechanisms of these models. The success in improving jailbreaking and reasoning suggests practical applications for alignment and model optimization.
*   **Comparison against prior art** The contribution and comparison againt prior art are well-summarized:
    *   SAMD addresses the gap that previous attribution research primarily focused on multi-layer perceptron (MLP) neurons and addressed relatively simple concepts such as factual associations.
    *   SAMD is the first to performs attention head attribution plus intervention using arbitrary concepts
    *   Default SAMI configuration outperforms SAE vector intervention, which requires extensive sweep to determine the optimal intervention coefficient

*   **Reproducibility and Openness:**  While the paper provides sufficient details for reproducibility, releasing code and data would greatly enhance its impact and enable further research.

**Justification for Score:**

This paper is a solid contribution to the field. The SAMD/SAMI pipeline offers a valuable new method for understanding and controlling transformer models. It is not a revolutionary breakthrough, but rather an incremental improvement on existing techniques. While the paper does have its limitations (e.g., lack of causal claims, potential hyperparameter sensitivity), the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. The paper offers a well-motivated, scalable, and domain-agnostic approach supported by compelling empirical evidence.
The novelty and utility combined with the well-supported experimental methodology make this a solid contribution to the field.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Generative Modeling of Full-Atom Protein Conformations using Latent Diffusion on Graph Embeddings
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17064v1)
- **Authors**: Aditya Sengar, Ali Hariri, Daniel Probst, Patrick Barth, Pierre Vandergheynst
- **Abstract**: Generating diverse, all-atom conformational ensembles of dynamic proteins such as G-protein-coupled receptors (GPCRs) is critical for understanding their function, yet most generative models simplify atomic detail or ignore conformational diversity altogether. We present latent diffusion for full protein generation (LD-FPG), a framework that constructs complete all-atom protein structures, including every side-chain heavy atom, directly from molecular dynamics (MD) trajectories. LD-FPG employs a Chebyshev graph neural network (ChebNet) to obtain low-dimensional latent embeddings of protein conformations, which are processed using three pooling strategies: blind, sequential and residue-based. A diffusion model trained on these latent representations generates new samples that a decoder, optionally regularized by dihedral-angle losses, maps back to Cartesian coordinates. Using D2R-MD, a 2-microsecond MD trajectory (12 000 frames) of the human dopamine D2 receptor in a membrane environment, the sequential and residue-based pooling strategy reproduces the reference ensemble with high structural fidelity (all-atom lDDT of approximately 0.7; C-alpha-lDDT of approximately 0.8) and recovers backbone and side-chain dihedral-angle distributions with a Jensen-Shannon divergence of less than 0.03 compared to the MD data. LD-FPG thereby offers a practical route to system-specific, all-atom ensemble generation for large proteins, providing a promising tool for structure-based therapeutic design on complex, dynamic targets. The D2R-MD dataset and our implementation are freely available to facilitate further research.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Latent Diffusion for Full Protein Generation (LD-FPG), a novel framework for generating diverse, all-atom protein conformations from molecular dynamics (MD) trajectory data. LD-FPG uses a Chebyshev graph neural network (ChebNet) to create low-dimensional latent embeddings of protein conformations, which are then processed using different pooling strategies (blind, sequential, residue-based). A diffusion model is trained on these latent representations, generating new samples that a decoder maps back to Cartesian coordinates. The approach is tested on a 2 µs MD trajectory of the human dopamine D2 receptor (D2R), demonstrating the ability to reproduce the reference ensemble with high structural fidelity, particularly with residue-based pooling.  The code and the D2R-MD dataset are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel combination of existing techniques (graph neural networks, diffusion models, latent embeddings) tailored explicitly for the all-atom generation of protein conformational ensembles. While individual components are not new, the integration into LD-FPG and the specific application to protein dynamics (particularly GPCRs) constitutes a non-trivial advance. The focus on capturing side-chain dynamics, a common weakness of other generative models, adds to the novelty. Furthermore, the evaluation of different decoder pooling strategies contributes new insights into ensemble generation. To the best of the authors' knowledge, this represents the first diffusion modeling framework aimed to capture both backbone and sidechain dynamics directly from MD simulations.

*   **Significance:** The ability to generate accurate and diverse conformational ensembles of dynamic proteins like GPCRs is critical for understanding protein function and guiding therapeutic design.  The all-atom detail generated by LD-FPG surpasses many existing methods that operate at the backbone or coarse-grained level.  The D2R case study showcases a practical application of the method to a pharmaceutically relevant target, indicating its potential impact on structure-based drug discovery. The system-specific approach leveraging MD data offers a path to overcoming limitations of more generalized models for complex, dynamic targets like GPCRs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-structured presentation.
    *   Comprehensive evaluation of different pooling strategies, providing valuable insights.
    *   Demonstrated performance on a challenging and relevant target (D2R).
    *   Public availability of code and dataset promotes reproducibility and further research.
    *   Ablation studies regarding choices on decoder conditioning.
    *   Rigorous consideration of the environmental costs of training by outlining and analyzing the CO2e emmissions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Increased steric clashes were observed in generated structures which is still a significant limitation.
    *   The need for MD simulation data is a limitation, as it restricts the applicability of LD-FPG to proteins with existing, high-quality MD trajectories.  Generating MD data for every target is computationally expensive.
    *   Multi-epoch sampling for residue pooling makes it less efficient and requires futher optimization for complex structure systems.

*   **Potential Influence:** LD-FPG provides a promising tool for structure-based drug design and mechanistic studies of dynamic proteins. By enabling the generation of realistic conformational ensembles, it can help researchers understand protein function and design drugs targeting specific states or allosteric sites.  The framework also provides a template for developing similar models for other protein systems or biomolecules. The study raises interesting questions about the optimal trade-off between global and local representations in generating conformational ensembles.  The public release of code and data should facilitate wider adoption and further development of the method.

*   **Justification of Score:** While the paper builds upon existing techniques, its specific application to all-atom protein ensemble generation from MD data, the novel evaluation of pooling strategies, and the demonstration on a relevant GPCR target warrants a high score. The current limitations concerning steric clashes and reliance on MD data, along with the other weaknesses, holds it back from achieving the highest possible score.  The clarity of presentation and release of code and data strengthen its significance.

Score: 7.5

### Empowering Near-Field Communications in Low-Altitude Economy with LLM: Fundamentals, Potentials, Solutions, and Future Directions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17067v1)
- **Authors**: Zhuo Xu, Tianyue Zheng, Linglong Dai
- **Abstract**: The low-altitude economy (LAE) is gaining significant attention from academia and industry. Fortunately, LAE naturally aligns with near-field communications in extremely large-scale MIMO (XL-MIMO) systems. By leveraging near-field beamfocusing, LAE can precisely direct beam energy to unmanned aerial vehicles, while the additional distance dimension boosts overall spectrum efficiency. However, near-field communications in LAE still face several challenges, such as the increase in signal processing complexity and the necessity of distinguishing between far and near-field users. Inspired by the large language models (LLM) with powerful ability to handle complex problems, we apply LLM to solve challenges of near-field communications in LAE. The objective of this article is to provide a comprehensive analysis and discussion on LLM-empowered near-field communications in LAE. Specifically, we first introduce fundamentals of LLM and near-field communications, including the key advantages of LLM and key characteristics of near-field communications. Then, we reveal the opportunities and challenges of near-field communications in LAE. To address these challenges, we present a LLM-based scheme for near-field communications in LAE, and provide a case study which jointly distinguishes far and near-field users and designs multi-user precoding matrix. Finally, we outline and highlight several future research directions and open issues.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) to address the challenges of near-field communications in the Low-Altitude Economy (LAE). LAE leverages unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) for various applications. The paper highlights the natural synergy between LAE and near-field communications using extremely large-scale MIMO (XL-MIMO), where near-field beamfocusing can precisely direct energy to UAVs. However, near-field communications in LAE face challenges like increased signal processing complexity and the need to distinguish between far and near-field users.  The authors propose leveraging the capabilities of LLMs to solve these challenges. The paper introduces the fundamentals of LLMs and near-field communications, reveals opportunities and challenges of this integration, proposes an LLM-based scheme for near-field communication in LAE, provides a case study on user classification and precoding, and outlines future research directions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Timeliness and Relevance:** The paper addresses a highly relevant and emerging area, combining two hot topics: LLMs and LAE. The focus on near-field communications in this context is a novel perspective.
*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges faced by near-field communications in LAE, particularly the signal processing complexity and the need for user differentiation.
*   **Comprehensive Approach:** The paper covers a broad range of aspects, from the fundamentals of LLMs and near-field communications to potential solutions and future directions.
*   **Specific LLM-Based Scheme:** The proposal of a specific LLM-based framework, including the input module, pre-processing, LLM backbone, and output modules, demonstrates a practical approach.
*   **Case Study and Simulation:** Including a case study with simulation results (though details are somewhat limited in this abstract) provides initial validation for the proposed approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Technical Depth:** The paper lacks deep technical details. The simulation results presented in the figures appear rather generic with many benchmarks lacking the necessary technical analysis. More in-depth explanations of the LLM architecture used, specific fine-tuning techniques, and mathematical formulations would significantly improve the rigor. The explanation of multi-user precoding using the LLM also lacks concrete technical details.
*   **Novelty Concerns:** While the combination of LLMs and LAE is novel, the paper draws heavily on existing LLM methodologies. The proposed architecture of using input encoders, a transformer backbone, and output decoders is a standard pattern, calling into question the true technical innovation of the work.
*   **Limited Evaluation:** The simulation setups and performance metrics are not thoroughly explained.  A more detailed discussion of the baseline schemes and a comparative analysis of computational complexity are needed. The performance gains are mentioned but without rigorous justification.
*   **Future directions are too broad:** The section on future directions lacks specificity. Suggesting "LLM-empowered multi-task networks" and "multi-modal LLMs" are high-level ideas, not concrete research directions. The authors should delve deeper into the potential applications, challenges, and unique designs needed to make these ideas viable.
*   **Lack of Experimental Validation:** The paper relies heavily on simulations. The performance of the algorithm has not been validated in actual physical testbeds, particularly in real-world environment conditions.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance stems from its potential to guide research in applying LLMs to solve specific problems in wireless communication within the context of LAE. However, without more substantial technical contributions and validation, its impact is limited.

**Justification of the Score:**

The paper has moderate novelty by combining LLMs with near-field communications in the LAE, which is a currently relevant field, but the technical depth and concrete contributions are relatively weak. The paper offers a reasonable overview and highlights potential directions, but it requires significant improvement in the technical details, experimental evaluation, and in presenting novel advancements beyond standard LLM methodologies to truly make a substantial contribution.

### Cross-Modal Epileptic Signal Harmonization: Frequency Domain Mapping Quantization for Pre-training a Unified Neurophysiological Transformer
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17068v1)
- **Authors**: Runkai Zhang, Hua Yu, John Q. Gan, Haixian Wang
- **Abstract**: Scalp electroencephalography (EEG) and intracranial EEG (iEEG) are vital for epilepsy diagnosis and treatment. Their unified analysis offers the potential to harness the complementary strengths of each modality but is challenging due to variations in recording montages, amplitude and signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), and frequency components. To address the aforementioned challenges, this paper introduces EpiNT, a novel Transformer-based pre-trained model for unified EEG and iEEG analysis. EpiNT employs channel-independent modeling with masked autoencoders (MAE) and vector quantization (VQ), along with a frequency domain mapping quantizer to capture crucial frequency features. Pre-trained on over 2,700 hours of multi-modal clinical neurophysiological data from 1,199 patients, EpiNT outperformed both randomly initialized models and other pre-trained methods on six downstream classification tasks, demonstrating robust representation learning capabilities. This work presents a promising approach for unified epilepsy neurophysiology analysis.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EpiNT (Epilepsy Neurophysiological Transformer), a novel Transformer-based pre-trained model designed for unified analysis of both scalp EEG and intracranial EEG (iEEG) data in epilepsy. EpiNT addresses the challenges posed by variations in recording montages, signal-to-noise ratio (SNR), and frequency components between these modalities.  It employs channel-independent modeling, masked autoencoders (MAE), vector quantization (VQ), and a frequency domain mapping quantizer.  The model is pre-trained on a large dataset of over 2,700 hours of EEG/iEEG recordings from 1,199 patients and is then fine-tuned on several downstream classification tasks related to epilepsy diagnosis and treatment. The results show that EpiNT outperforms both randomly initialized models and other pre-trained models, demonstrating robust representation learning capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents several novel aspects:

    *   **Unified EEG/iEEG Analysis:**  While pre-training on EEG data has been explored before, the explicit attempt to unify scalp EEG and iEEG analysis within a single model architecture, addressing the significant discrepancies between these modalities is a strong point. This is particularly important for clinical scenarios where both types of data might be available.

    *   **Frequency Domain Mapping Quantizer:** The introduction of a non-trainable frequency domain mapping quantizer is a novel approach to capture discrete spectral representations, addressing frequency content variability between scalp and intracranial recordings. This distinguishes it from other VQ-based methods and reconstruction methods that are done in the time-domain.

    *   **Combination of MAE and VQ:** The paper's hybrid pre-training strategy that combines MAE and VQ to enhance robustness to amplitude and SNR differences shows a carefully designed strategy.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Clinical Relevance:** The downstream tasks chosen for evaluation (seizure onset zone localization, seizure prediction, IED detection, pathology classification, seizure detection) are all highly relevant to clinical practice in epilepsy management. Demonstrating improved performance on these tasks could have a direct impact on patient care.

    *   **Large-Scale Dataset:**  The model is pre-trained on a substantial dataset of EEG/iEEG recordings, enhancing its generalizability and practical utility.

    *   **Ablation studies:** Thorough ablations studies showcase which components of the method are most important.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper rigorously evaluates EpiNT through extensive experimentation, comparing it to multiple baselines and exploring different fine-tuning strategies.
    *   **Interpretability Analysis:** The interpretability analysis, while preliminary, provides valuable insights into how EpiNT processes electrophysiological signals and learns relevant features. Comparing learned features to conventional EEG features helps in understanding what the model is actually learning.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvements:** Some of the performance gains over existing pre-trained models, while consistent, might be considered incremental. The reported Specificity and NPV scores being comparatively modest in certain scenarios is worth noting. However, the significance is driven by the combination of advances.
    *   **Black Box Nature:**  The use of a deep learning model makes it difficult to fully understand the precise mechanisms by which EpiNT achieves its performance.  The interpretability analysis offers some insights, but more detailed investigations are needed.
    *   **Limited Out-of-the-Box Comparison:** The paper's out-of-the-box comparison may suffer from not using the original pre-trained weights.

*   **Potential Impact:**
    EpiNT offers a promising approach for unified epilepsy neurophysiology analysis. Its ability to generalize across diverse acquisition settings and achieve strong performance on clinically relevant tasks could potentially streamline clinical workflows and improve patient outcomes. The modular design also allows for future integration of multimodal data (e.g. fMRI and genomics).
    The detailed ablation studies also provide insights to aid future model development for EEG/iEEG analysis.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper introduces a novel and well-engineered pre-trained model, EpiNT, for a clinically relevant problem: unified analysis of EEG and iEEG data in epilepsy. The innovations in its architecture, particularly the frequency domain mapping quantizer, are significant and the thorough evaluation demonstrates its potential. While the performance gains might be seen as incremental in some cases, the combination of techniques, the large-scale pre-training dataset, and clinical tasks establish its value. However, the black box nature of deep learning limits a full understanding of the model's inner workings and potential improvements in interpretability would strengthen the findings. The rigorous ablations and detailed performance analysis across different pre-training strategies makes the work impactful and valuable.

### Assembler: Scalable 3D Part Assembly via Anchor Point Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17074v1)
- **Authors**: Wang Zhao, Yan-Pei Cao, Jiale Xu, Yuejiang Dong, Ying Shan
- **Abstract**: We present Assembler, a scalable and generalizable framework for 3D part assembly that reconstructs complete objects from input part meshes and a reference image. Unlike prior approaches that mostly rely on deterministic part pose prediction and category-specific training, Assembler is designed to handle diverse, in-the-wild objects with varying part counts, geometries, and structures. It addresses the core challenges of scaling to general 3D part assembly through innovations in task formulation, representation, and data. First, Assembler casts part assembly as a generative problem and employs diffusion models to sample plausible configurations, effectively capturing ambiguities arising from symmetry, repeated parts, and multiple valid assemblies. Second, we introduce a novel shape-centric representation based on sparse anchor point clouds, enabling scalable generation in Euclidean space rather than SE(3) pose prediction. Third, we construct a large-scale dataset of over 320K diverse part-object assemblies using a synthesis and filtering pipeline built on existing 3D shape repositories. Assembler achieves state-of-the-art performance on PartNet and is the first to demonstrate high-quality assembly for complex, real-world objects. Based on Assembler, we further introduce an interesting part-aware 3D modeling system that generates high-resolution, editable objects from images, demonstrating potential for interactive and compositional design. Project page: https://assembler3d.github.io
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the "Assembler: Scalable 3D Part Assembly via Anchor Point Diffusion" paper.

**Summary**

The paper introduces Assembler, a novel framework for 3D part assembly, designed to reconstruct complete objects from input part meshes and a reference image.  The core idea is to formulate part assembly as a generative problem and employ diffusion models to sample plausible configurations.  The paper also introduces a novel shape-centric representation based on sparse anchor point clouds, allowing generation in Euclidean space rather than SE(3) pose prediction. To address the lack of large-scale data, a synthesis and filtering pipeline is employed to create a dataset of diverse part-object assemblies.  Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on PartNet and high-quality assembly for complex, real-world objects. Finally, the paper showcases a part-aware 3D modeling system that generates high-resolution, editable objects from images, highlighting the potential for interactive and compositional design.

**Critical Evaluation**

The paper tackles a crucial and challenging problem in 3D computer vision and graphics: scalable and generalizable part assembly. Previous methods have often been limited by category-specific training and reliance on deterministic pose prediction.  Assembler's key contributions lie in three areas: the generative formulation using diffusion models, the shape-centric representation using anchor points, and the creation of a large-scale dataset.

*   **Novelty:**
    *   The formulation of 3D part assembly as a generative task using diffusion models to handle ambiguity is a strong contribution. While diffusion models have been explored for related tasks like fracture assembly, their application to general part assembly, conditioned on both meshes and images, is novel.
    *   The sparse anchor point representation is a clever way to circumvent the challenges associated with direct pose prediction in SE(3). It provides a more shape-aware representation suitable for generative modeling.
    *   The data synthesis pipeline, though not entirely groundbreaking, addresses a significant bottleneck in the field. Generating a large and diverse dataset is crucial for training generalizable models.

*   **Significance:**
    *   Achieving state-of-the-art results on PartNet, a standard benchmark, is a good validation of the approach.
    *   The ability to assemble complex, real-world objects, as demonstrated in the figures, represents a significant step forward compared to existing methods that are often limited to simple, canonical shapes.
    *   The exploration of part-aware 3D modeling based on Assembler opens interesting avenues for interactive and compositional design.
    *   The release of the dataset will likely benefit the research community and accelerate progress in 3D part assembly.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
    *   Technically sound approach with innovative components.
    *   Comprehensive experiments demonstrating superior performance.
    *   Good qualitative results showing the model's capabilities.
    *   Addresses a real-world need for scalable 3D content creation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The diffusion model, while effective, could be computationally expensive for complex scenes.
    *   The data synthesis pipeline, while helpful, might introduce biases that could affect the model's generalization ability on truly unseen, real-world data.
    *   The paper mentions limitations in handling scenarios with numerous small parts and precise boundary alignments, indicating areas for future improvement.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more in-depth ablation study to isolate the impact of each component (e.g., the impact of the exact configuration of the diffusion model) more thoroughly.

Overall, this is a solid paper that makes significant contributions to the field of 3D part assembly. It introduces novel techniques, demonstrates strong results, and provides a valuable resource for the research community.

### Chain-of-Thought Prompting Obscures Hallucination Cues in Large Language Models: An Empirical Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17088v1)
- **Authors**: Jiahao Cheng, Tiancheng Su, Jia Yuan, Guoxiu He, Jiawei Liu, Xinqi Tao, Jingwen Xie, Huaxia Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) often exhibit \textit{hallucinations}, generating factually incorrect or semantically irrelevant content in response to prompts. Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting can mitigate hallucinations by encouraging step-by-step reasoning, but its impact on hallucination detection remains underexplored. To bridge this gap, we conduct a systematic empirical evaluation. We begin with a pilot experiment, revealing that CoT reasoning significantly affects the LLM's internal states and token probability distributions. Building on this, we evaluate the impact of various CoT prompting methods on mainstream hallucination detection methods across both instruction-tuned and reasoning-oriented LLMs. Specifically, we examine three key dimensions: changes in hallucination score distributions, variations in detection accuracy, and shifts in detection confidence. Our findings show that while CoT prompting helps reduce hallucination frequency, it also tends to obscure critical signals used for detection, impairing the effectiveness of various detection methods. Our study highlights an overlooked trade-off in the use of reasoning. Code is publicly available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/cot-hallu-detect.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Chain-of-Thought Prompting Obscures Hallucination Cues in Large Language Models: An Empirical Evaluation" investigates the interaction between chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting and hallucination detection in large language models (LLMs). While CoT prompting is known to improve LLM performance and reduce hallucination *frequency*, the paper argues that it can simultaneously *obscure* cues used by hallucination detection methods, thus making it harder to identify remaining hallucinations. Through extensive experiments across various datasets, LLMs, and hallucination detection techniques, the authors demonstrate a dual effect: CoT enhances performance but impairs the effectiveness of some detection methods.  Internal-state-based and self-evaluation-based detection methods are shown to be particularly vulnerable to CoT's obfuscation, while consistency-based methods are more robust.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a crucial and previously underexplored aspect of LLM reliability: the impact of reasoning-based prompting on *detectability* of hallucinations, as opposed to just hallucination frequency.  This is a nuanced but important distinction. While others have examined CoT's effect on accuracy, the focus on how it influences the signals available for *detecting* errors is a novel contribution.
*   **Significance:**  The findings have significant implications for the responsible use of LLMs. If CoT prompting makes it harder to detect hallucinations, it undermines efforts to build reliable AI systems, especially in sensitive domains (healthcare, legal, etc.).  The paper highlights a trade-off that practitioners must be aware of. The research also points towards the need for new hallucination detection strategies that are robust to reasoning-enhanced LLM outputs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive experimental design, covering multiple datasets (question answering, summarization), LLMs (both instruction-tuned and reasoning-oriented), CoT prompting methods, and hallucination detection techniques. This broad scope strengthens the conclusions.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of CoT obscuring hallucination cues, providing concrete examples to illustrate the issue.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The authors delve into the mechanisms behind the phenomenon, examining how CoT impacts token probability distributions and internal states within the LLMs. This analysis provides valuable insights into the interplay between reasoning and error detection.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper directly addresses the implications for the applied use of large language models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** While the experimental setup is broad, it's still limited to a specific set of open-source LLMs, datasets, and detection methods. Expanding the research to include closed-source models (e.g., GPT-4) and a wider range of tasks would further strengthen the findings.
    *   **Reliance on Standard Metrics:**  The paper uses metrics like Exact Match and ROUGE-L for assessing response correctness. While common, these metrics might not fully capture the nuances of semantic accuracy and relevance. The use of more specialized metrics could provide a deeper understanding of the types of hallucinations that are being missed.
    *   **Lack of Explanations for Consistency-Based Robustness:** The paper observes that consistency-based methods are more robust, but it doesn't fully explain *why*.  Further investigation into the underlying mechanisms could provide valuable insights for developing more resilient detection approaches.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to influence future research in hallucination detection, prompting the development of methods that are more resilient to CoT-induced obfuscation. It also raises important considerations for the design of prompts intended for real-world applications. Furthermore, its focus on the internal states could foster studies to probe and leverage the internal representations of LLMs for more faithful and truthful text generation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper offers a novel and significant contribution to the field. While there are some limitations regarding the scope of experiments and the metrics used, the core finding – that CoT prompting can negatively impact hallucination *detection* – is well-supported and has practical implications. The paper successfully identifies and elucidates an important trade-off in the use of LLMs, stimulating new research directions in both hallucination detection and prompt engineering. The analysis is detailed and provides useful insights, and its emphasis on internal states is a strong point. It's a solid advance in understanding LLM reliability.

### Towards Advanced Mathematical Reasoning for LLMs via First-Order Logic Theorem Proving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17104v1)
- **Authors**: Chuxue Cao, Mengze Li, Juntao Dai, Jinluan Yang, Zijian Zhao, Shengyu Zhang, Weijie Shi, Chengzhong Liu, Sirui Han, Yike Guo
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown promising first-order logic (FOL) reasoning capabilities with applications in various areas. However, their effectiveness in complex mathematical reasoning involving multi-step FOL deductions is still under-researched. While LLMs perform competitively on established mathematical reasoning benchmarks, they struggle with multi-step FOL tasks, as demonstrated by Deepseek-Prover-V2-7B's low accuracy (4.2%) on our proposed theorem proving dataset. This issue arises from the limited exploration of diverse proof strategies and the potential for early reasoning mistakes to undermine entire proofs. To address these issues, we propose DREAM, a self-adaptive solution that enhances the Diversity and REAsonability of LLMs' generation strategies. DREAM incorporates an Axiom-Driven Strategy Diversification mechanism to promote varied strategic outcomes and a Sub-Proposition Error Feedback to help LLMs reflect on and correct their proofs. Our contributions include pioneering advancements in LLMs' mathematical reasoning through FOL theorem proving, introducing a novel inference stage solution that improves performance by 0.6% to 6.4%, and providing a curated dataset of 447 mathematical theorems in Lean 4 format for evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DREAM, a novel inference-stage solution designed to improve the mathematical reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) within the context of First-Order Logic (FOL) theorem proving. The core problem addressed is the LLMs' difficulty with multi-step FOL deductions, even when pre-trained on formal mathematics corpora. DREAM tackles this through two primary mechanisms:

1.  **Axiom-Driven Strategy Diversification:** Promotes exploration of diverse proof strategies using a k-wise combinatorial axiom tree.  This combats the issue of strategy starvation where LLMs get stuck in repetitive, often unproductive, lines of reasoning.
2.  **Sub-Proposition Error Feedback:** Mitigates cascading errors by aligning error messages from the formal compiler with the corresponding sub-propositions in the proof, enabling more targeted and effective self-correction.

The paper also presents a curated dataset of 447 mathematical theorems in Lean 4 format for evaluation. Experiments demonstrate that DREAM improves the performance of LLMs on FOL theorem proving tasks, achieving gains over baseline methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies in its specific focus on improving LLMs' performance in *FOL theorem proving* tasks, a domain where existing benchmarks and methods are less well-developed. The combination of axiom-driven strategy diversification and sub-proposition error feedback is a novel approach to addressing the specific challenges of FOL deduction, such as the sensitivity to initial assumptions and the propagation of errors.  While prior work has explored inference-stage improvements and error feedback, DREAM's adaptation of these concepts to the intricacies of FOL proving appears unique. Constructing a FOL specific dataset for evaluation purposes adds additional value.

**Significance:**

The significance of the work resides in pushing LLMs towards more rigorous and formal reasoning. Improving LLMs' FOL capabilities has potential implications for areas requiring precise logical deduction and verification. The performance improvements, while not earth-shattering, demonstrate a tangible advance in a challenging domain. The curated dataset provides a valuable resource for future research in this area.

**Strengths:**

*   **Targeted Approach:** The paper specifically addresses the challenges of FOL theorem proving, rather than treating it as a generic reasoning problem.
*   **Well-Defined Mechanisms:** The axiom-driven strategy diversification and sub-proposition error feedback are clearly articulated and justified.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The paper provides experimental results comparing DREAM to baselines, demonstrating its effectiveness.
*   **Dataset Contribution:** The curated dataset is a valuable contribution to the field, filling a gap in available resources for FOL theorem proving.
* The work carefully decomposes the difficulties of FOL-based reasoning into Strategy Starvation and Severe Cascading Error providing a reasonable framework to address this area.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** While the focus on FOL is a strength, it also limits the generalizability of the findings.  It is not clear how well DREAM would translate to other types of reasoning tasks.
*   **Incremental Improvement:** The performance gains, while consistent, are relatively modest.  The absolute performance on the FOL theorem proving task remains low, suggesting further improvements are needed.
*   **Dependency on Formal Compiler:** The sub-proposition error feedback relies on a formal compiler providing error messages. This limits applicability in situations without such a compiler.
*   **Computational Cost:** The iterative refinement process and the construction of axiom trees could be computationally expensive. A clear articulation of the trade-offs between increased accuracy and computational resources should be provided.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a clear understanding of the challenges involved in FOL reasoning and provides a novel and targeted solution to address these challenges. The experimental results, while not groundbreaking, demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method. The construction of a curated dataset is a valuable contribution to the field. However, the relatively modest performance gains and limited scope limit the paper's overall impact.
