### Are Bias Evaluation Methods Biased ?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17111v1)
- **Authors**: Lina Berrayana, Sean Rooney, Luis Garcés-Erice, Ioana Giurgiu
- **Abstract**: The creation of benchmarks to evaluate the safety of Large Language Models is one of the key activities within the trusted AI community. These benchmarks allow models to be compared for different aspects of safety such as toxicity, bias, harmful behavior etc. Independent benchmarks adopt different approaches with distinct data sets and evaluation methods. We investigate how robust such benchmarks are by using different approaches to rank a set of representative models for bias and compare how similar are the overall rankings. We show that different but widely used bias evaluations methods result in disparate model rankings. We conclude with recommendations for the community in the usage of such benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether different bias evaluation methods for Large Language Models (LLMs) lead to consistent model rankings. The authors compare three widely used, but methodologically distinct, approaches: structured question-answering datasets (BBQ), LLM-as-a-judge, and sentiment-based evaluation. They control for potential confounding factors like the number of templates, demographic categories, specific groups included, and evaluation set size to ensure a fair comparison. The study reveals that the methods yield significantly different model rankings, suggesting that the choice of bias evaluation method can heavily influence conclusions about model bias. The authors suggest that these discrepancies may stem from human subjectivity and model-specific biases inherent in the evaluation frameworks themselves and recommend a critical approach when interpreting results and combining different methods, they propose that comparing the rankings, rather than relying on absolute scores, offers a more meaningful comparison.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Focus on Model Rankings:** The paper correctly highlights the importance of model rankings in practical scenarios (e.g., enterprise model selection) over absolute bias scores. This is a more practical viewpoint than solely focusing on numerical bias scores.
    *   **Controlled Comparison:** The authors take care to eliminate several sources of variation in the experimental setup, making the comparison of the bias evaluation methods more rigorous than previous studies.
    *   **Identifies potential confounding factors:** The paper identifies and discusses potential biases present in each of the three bias evaluation techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope:** The study evaluates only three bias evaluation methods. While these are representative, exploring a wider range of methods would provide a more comprehensive understanding of the issue.
    *   **Subjectivity in Thresholds:** The choice of the threshold in Section 4.2 for deeming a response as biased introduces a degree of subjectivity and might influence the results. A sensitivity analysis of the influence of that threshold would be beneficial.
    *   **Model Selection:** The choice of LLMs used for evaluation, while including popular options, could be expanded to encompass more recent frontier models like GPT-4, Claude, or Gemini.
    *   **Limited Explanation of Discrepancies:** While the paper identifies discrepancies in rankings, the explanations for why these occur are sometimes speculative. A deeper dive into the specific examples and characteristics of each model that drive these ranking differences would add substantial value. For example a more in-depth investigation on the tendency of 1lama-3-1-8b-instruct to provide cautious responses is needed.

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the direct comparison of fundamentally different bias evaluation methods *under controlled conditions*, focusing on the resulting model *rankings* rather than just individual bias scores. This approach is relatively less explored in existing literature. While previous studies have compared bias evaluation methods, they often lack a rigorously controlled experimental setup with identical bias categories or evaluation set sizes.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant because it reveals a crucial paradox: benchmarks designed to detect bias may themselves be biased, significantly impacting model selection and deployment. The insights presented highlight the need for critical awareness when using bias evaluation methods and promote a more nuanced interpretation of bias rankings, especially in an industrial context where model rankings guide decisions. The paper suggests combining methods and prioritizing relative rankings over absolute scores which is important advice for the field.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence how organizations evaluate and select LLMs for specific applications. By emphasizing the instability of bias evaluations and advocating for a more critical approach, it could encourage the development of more robust and reliable bias assessment methodologies. It could also drive research into combining different evaluation approaches to mitigate the limitations of individual methods.

**Overall:** While the paper has some limitations in scope and depth of analysis, it makes a valuable contribution to the field by directly comparing different bias evaluation techniques under controlled conditions and showing that very different model rankings result from the choice of bias measurement methods. The emphasis on rankings versus absolute scores is also a practical and valuable insight for the community.

### When Can Model-Free Reinforcement Learning be Enough for Thinking?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17124v1)
- **Authors**: Josiah P. Hanna, Nicholas E. Corrado
- **Abstract**: Recent work on large language models has demonstrated the use of model-free reinforcement learning (RL) to train reasoning-like capabilities. The emergence of "thinking" through model-free RL is interesting as thinking actions neither produce reward nor change the external world state to one where the agent is more likely to get reward. This paper seeks to build a domain-independent understanding of when model-free RL will lead to "thinking" as a strategy for reward maximization. To build this understanding, we first introduce a theoretical model which we call a \textit{thought Markov decision process} (MDP). Thought MDPs minimally extend the classical MDP model to include an abstract notion of thought state and thought action. Using the thought MDP model, we prove the importance of policy initialization in determining whether or not thinking emerges and show formally that thought actions are equivalent to the agent choosing to perform a step of policy improvement before continuing to act. We then show that open-source LLMs satisfy the conditions that our theory predicts are necessary for model-free RL to produce thinking-like behavior. Finally, we hypothesize sufficient conditions that would enable thinking to be learned outside of language generation and introduce a toy domain where a combination of multi-task pre-training and designated thought actions enable more data-efficient RL compared to non-thinking agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the conditions under which model-free reinforcement learning (RL) agents will develop "thinking" behaviors, defined as actions that don't directly produce reward or change the external environment but improve future reward acquisition. The authors introduce the "thought MDP" framework, a formal extension of the classic MDP that explicitly models thought states and actions. Using this framework, they prove that policy initialization plays a crucial role in the emergence of thinking and that thought actions can be interpreted as a local policy improvement step. They connect their theory to large language models (LLMs), showing how chain-of-thought prompting relates to the thought MDP framework and providing evidence that such prompts increase the expected return.  Finally, they present a simple gridworld domain with multi-task pre-training where model-free RL discovers thinking behavior.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's main novelty lies in formalizing the notion of "thinking" within a standard RL framework using the thought MDP.  Prior work has explored related ideas like decision-time planning and learning to plan, but the thought MDP provides a more abstract, domain-independent lens for examining the conditions that lead to thinking. Another novel aspect is the link between thought MDPs and the options framework and exploration of the relationship to the effective horizon in MDPs. The empirical demonstrations, while not groundbreaking in their own right, serve to ground the theoretical results and illustrate their applicability to LLMs and the gridworld experiment is a good proof of concept.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is in providing a framework for understanding and potentially designing RL agents capable of more complex, deliberative behaviors. By identifying the importance of policy initialization and framing thinking as local policy improvement, the authors offer a new perspective on how to train agents that exhibit reasoning-like capabilities. Connecting this work to LLMs is important as this is a particularly salient area of current research. The gridworld experiment provides an avenue for future exploration.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The theoretical framework is well-defined and rigorous, with clear definitions and proofs.
    *   The connection to LLMs is relevant and helps to contextualize the theoretical results in a real-world setting.
    *   The illustrative examples are helpful for understanding the concepts.
    *   The gridworld provides an experimental avenue for future exploration.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The thought MDP model relies on the assumption of deterministic thought transitions, which may not hold in all cases. This simplifies the analysis but limits the generality of the results.
    *   While the paper explains *when* thinking might emerge, it doesn't fully address *how* to design the thought states and actions in novel environments. The gridworld experiment offers some insights, but more work is needed to develop a general methodology for creating effective thought MDPs.
    *   The experiments, particularly the LLM validation, are somewhat limited in scope. A more thorough empirical analysis would strengthen the paper.
    *   Some assumptions are made for ease of analysis, like non-negative rewards, but could be relaxed for more general applicability.
    *   There are still some very fundamental open questions that would impact the strength of the argument if resolved; in particular, "Where do thought MDPs come from?"

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in RL by providing a new formal framework for thinking. It could lead to the development of new algorithms and architectures for training agents that exhibit more complex reasoning and planning abilities. The connection to LLMs could also inspire new approaches to training language models to perform more deliberative tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel theoretical framework and offers a promising perspective on the emergence of thinking in RL agents. While there are some limitations in the generality of the model and the scope of the empirical validation, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by providing a formal definition of "thinking" and identifying key factors that influence its emergence.

### Dynamic Watermark Generation for Digital Images using Perimeter Gated SPAD Imager PUFs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17134v1)
- **Authors**: Md Sakibur Sajal, Marc Dandin
- **Abstract**: Digital image watermarks as a security feature can be derived from the imager's physically unclonable functions (PUFs) by utilizing the manufacturing variations, i.e., the dark signal non-uniformity (DSNU). While a few demonstrations focused on the CMOS image sensors (CIS) and active pixel sensors (APS), single photon avalanche diode (SPAD) imagers have never been investigated for this purpose. In this work, we have proposed a novel watermarking technique using perimeter gated SPAD (pgSPAD) imagers. We utilized the DSNU of three 64 x 64 pgSPAD imager chips, fabricated in a 0.35 {\mu}m standard CMOS process and analyzed the simulated watermarks for standard test images from publicly available database. Our observation shows that both source identification and tamper detection can be achieved using the proposed source-scene-specific dynamic watermarks with a controllable sensitivity-robustness trade-off.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a new dynamic watermarking technique for digital images using the Physically Unclonable Functions (PUFs) derived from perimeter-gated Single Photon Avalanche Diode (pgSPAD) imagers.  It leverages the dark signal non-uniformity (DSNU) of pgSPAD imager arrays as the source of PUF.  The method involves generating binary feature images from the input image, constructing a challenge matrix from these features, and using this matrix to elicit a response from the pgSPAD imager (acting as a PUF). This response, combined with a fingerprint of the imager and the original challenge, forms the watermark. The paper demonstrates that this technique can be used for both source identification and tamper detection.  The paper also addresses robustness against noise by incorporating a double thresholding technique during the feature image generation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in applying PUFs derived from pgSPAD imagers to the domain of image watermarking.  While image sensor PUFs (using CMOS or APS) have been explored before, using pgSPADs, with their reconfigurable DSNU through perimeter gating, adds a new dimension. The construction of the dynamic watermark, incorporating challenge, response and device fingerprint, is a novel combination of known concepts.
* **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential of pgSPADs in future imaging applications. If pgSPADs become more widespread, this work offers a viable security mechanism leveraging the inherent manufacturing variations of the sensor. The temperature resilient mechanism and the reconfigurability of the PUF is also significant.
* **Strengths:**
    * The paper clearly explains the proposed method and its various components.
    * The use of publicly available images for testing adds credibility to the results.
    * The inclusion of tamper detection and robustness analysis demonstrates the practical applicability of the technique.
    * The discussion of the trade-off between sensitivity and robustness is a valuable contribution.
    * The qualitative comparison with other PUF-based methods help position the contribution in context.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * The experimental results, while promising, are based on a limited number of pgSPAD imager chips (three). A larger dataset would strengthen the claims.
    * While the paper mentions standard steganographic techniques for embedding the watermark, it does not delve deeply into the specific embedding method used or its impact on image quality.
    * The paper could benefit from a more in-depth comparison with existing watermarking techniques in terms of performance metrics like imperceptibility, capacity, and robustness against various attacks. While the paper addresses noise robustness, it doesn't consider other common attacks such as geometric distortions or compression.
    * The current demonstration relies on native DSNU. There's room to more fully leverage the perimeter gating for the PUF challenge and response which could further enhance the challenge-response-pair space.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel application of pgSPAD imagers for image watermarking. While building upon existing concepts of image sensor PUFs, the use of pgSPADs and the demonstrated tamper detection and noise robustness add a worthwhile contribution to the field. However, the limited experimental data and lack of extensive comparison with existing watermarking techniques prevent a higher score. The method also relies on native DSNU without fully leveraging the perimeter gate to its potential.

### Consistent Sampling and Simulation: Molecular Dynamics with Energy-Based Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17139v1)
- **Authors**: Michael Plainer, Hao Wu, Leon Klein, Stephan Günnemann, Frank Noé
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have recently gained significant attention due to their effectiveness in various scientific domains, including biochemistry. When trained on equilibrium molecular distributions, diffusion models provide both: a generative procedure to sample equilibrium conformations and associated forces derived from the model's scores. However, using the forces for coarse-grained molecular dynamics simulations uncovers inconsistencies in the samples generated via classical diffusion inference and simulation, despite both originating from the same model. Particularly at the small diffusion timesteps required for simulations, diffusion models fail to satisfy the Fokker-Planck equation, which governs how the score should evolve over time. We interpret this deviation as an indication of the observed inconsistencies and propose an energy-based diffusion model with a Fokker-Planck-derived regularization term enforcing consistency. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach on toy systems, alanine dipeptide, and introduce a state-of-the-art transferable Boltzmann emulator for dipeptides that supports simulation and demonstrates enhanced consistency and efficient sampling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the inconsistency between independent sampling and molecular dynamics (MD) simulations when using diffusion models trained on equilibrium molecular distributions. It identifies that diffusion models, particularly at small diffusion timesteps, often fail to satisfy the Fokker-Planck equation, leading to discrepancies between samples generated through diffusion inference and MD simulations. To address this, the authors propose an energy-based diffusion model with a Fokker-Planck-derived regularization term to enforce consistency.  They demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach on toy systems, alanine dipeptide, and introduce a transferable Boltzmann emulator for dipeptides, showing enhanced consistency and efficient sampling. The paper also explores using a mixture of experts (MoE) approach to further improve model capacity and consistency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the application of Fokker-Planck regularization to energy-based diffusion models specifically for improving the consistency between independent sampling and molecular dynamics simulations. Prior work has identified inconsistencies in diffusion models and proposed Fokker-Planck-inspired regularizations, but this paper focuses on addressing the specific needs of MD simulations, particularly the need for accurate forces derived from the model. The use of a weak residual formulation for the Fokker-Planck regularization to reduce computational cost is also a valuable contribution. The adaptive time-based MoE approach adds another layer of efficiency and targeted regularization.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the growing interest in using diffusion models for molecular simulations, which offer a promising avenue for generating equilibrium conformations and accessing thermodynamic properties. Inconsistencies between sampling and simulation limit the reliability of these models for detailed analysis and prediction. By improving consistency, this paper makes a step towards more reliable and physically meaningful MD simulations using diffusion models. The development of a transferable Boltzmann emulator for dipeptides is a valuable practical outcome, as it demonstrates the scalability of the proposed approach to relevant molecular systems. The explicit derivation of the energy and force relationships is also useful.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of inconsistencies between diffusion sampling and MD simulation.
    *   **Sound Methodology:** The proposed Fokker-Planck regularization is theoretically grounded and addresses the identified limitations of existing diffusion models. The derivation of the weak residual formulation is a practical and important contribution.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The approach is rigorously evaluated on various systems, from simple toy models to more complex molecular systems like alanine dipeptide and transferable dipeptides, with well defined metrics.
    *   **Practical Contribution:** The transferable Boltzmann emulator is a valuable outcome that demonstrates the potential for real-world applications.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper acknowledges the computational overhead introduced by Fokker-Planck regularization, even with the weak residual formulation. Further optimization and efficiency improvements would be beneficial. The use of MoE partially addresses this, but the implementation is described as not fully optimized.
    *   **Empirical Focus:** While theoretically motivated, the results are primarily empirical. A more rigorous theoretical analysis of the convergence properties and error bounds of the proposed method would strengthen the work.
    *   **Limited Scope:** While the transferable model is demonstrated on dipeptides, scaling this approach to larger and more complex molecular systems will likely present significant challenges.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of molecular simulations by enabling more reliable and consistent MD simulations using diffusion models. The proposed regularization technique could be adopted by other researchers working on diffusion-based molecular simulations. The transferable Boltzmann emulator could also find immediate practical applications.

*   **Score:** 7

*   **Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to address a significant problem in the field of diffusion-based molecular simulations. The use of Fokker-Planck regularization is theoretically sound and the empirical results demonstrate clear improvements in consistency. The creation of a transferable Boltzmann emulator further enhances the practical value of the work. While the computational cost and empirical focus limit the score, the overall contribution is significant and has the potential to make a valuable contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### MeDi: Metadata-Guided Diffusion Models for Mitigating Biases in Tumor Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17140v1)
- **Authors**: David Jacob Drexlin, Jonas Dippel, Julius Hense, Niklas Prenißl, Grégoire Montavon, Frederick Klauschen, Klaus-Robert Müller
- **Abstract**: Deep learning models have made significant advances in histological prediction tasks in recent years. However, for adaptation in clinical practice, their lack of robustness to varying conditions such as staining, scanner, hospital, and demographics is still a limiting factor: if trained on overrepresented subpopulations, models regularly struggle with less frequent patterns, leading to shortcut learning and biased predictions. Large-scale foundation models have not fully eliminated this issue. Therefore, we propose a novel approach explicitly modeling such metadata into a Metadata-guided generative Diffusion model framework (MeDi). MeDi allows for a targeted augmentation of underrepresented subpopulations with synthetic data, which balances limited training data and mitigates biases in downstream models. We experimentally show that MeDi generates high-quality histopathology images for unseen subpopulations in TCGA, boosts the overall fidelity of the generated images, and enables improvements in performance for downstream classifiers on datasets with subpopulation shifts. Our work is a proof-of-concept towards better mitigating data biases with generative models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "MeDi: Metadata-Guided Diffusion Models for Mitigating Biases in Tumor Classification" introduces a novel approach to address biases in histopathological image analysis using metadata-guided generative diffusion models (MeDi). The authors argue that standard deep learning models, including large foundation models, often struggle with varying conditions like staining protocols, scanner types, and demographic differences, leading to shortcut learning and biased predictions.  MeDi explicitly models these metadata attributes during the image generation process, allowing for targeted augmentation of underrepresented subpopulations with synthetic data. The authors demonstrate that MeDi generates higher-quality histopathology images compared to class-only conditioning and improves downstream classifier performance on datasets with subpopulation shifts. The core idea is to condition the diffusion model not only on the cancer subtype but also on readily available metadata like medical center, allowing the model to learn and generate more representative data for various subgroups. The code and pipeline are made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of using metadata to guide generative models for bias mitigation in histopathology is novel. Previous work has addressed domain adaptation via style transfer or stain normalization, and some diffusion models have explored conditioning on clustered morphological features. MeDi distinguishes itself by explicitly modeling metadata like medical center during generation, allowing for targeted augmentation of specific subpopulations. While the underlying diffusion model architecture itself is based on existing work, the integration of metadata in this specific way for bias mitigation in histopathology classification *is* a novel aspect. The authors also provide a clear justification for how this approach differs and builds upon existing literature, which is a positive element.

*   **Significance:** Bias is a significant challenge in medical image analysis, and the potential of MeDi to mitigate these biases is important. The experiments provide evidence that MeDi can improve the fidelity of generated images (lower FID scores) and improve downstream classifier performance on tasks affected by subpopulation shifts.  Demonstrating this improvement on the TCGA-UT dataset, which is multi-centric and multi-class (32 cancer types), strengthens the significance of the work. The public availability of the code enhances reproducibility and facilitates further research. This is an important aspect. The work can contribute to more reliable and generalizable AI systems in digital pathology.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Novel approach to explicitly model metadata in a diffusion model for bias mitigation.
    *   Experimental results demonstrating improved image fidelity and downstream task performance on TCGA-UT dataset.
    *   Publicly available code.
    *   Well presented and justified differences from related work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The improvement in performance is somewhat marginal (although it is stated to be significant) and the results only cover three cancer subtyping problems. It remains to be seen whether it can be broadly applicable.
    *   The specific metadata chosen (medical center) might not be the most important for all datasets/tasks. The effectiveness of the approach likely depends on the relevance of the chosen metadata to the specific biases present in the data. More evidence of different metadata factors working is needed.
    *   The use of synthetic data is not a perfect solution and requires care. There is a risk of reinforcing biases or introducing artifacts if the generative model is not carefully trained and evaluated.
    *   The method relies on the availability of well-annotated metadata. In many real-world scenarios, metadata may be incomplete or inaccurate, limiting the applicability of the approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by encouraging researchers to consider metadata when training generative models for medical image analysis. It provides a concrete example of how metadata can be integrated into a diffusion model to mitigate biases and improve generalization. Further research building upon this work could explore different metadata factors, larger-scale training regimes, and more sophisticated evaluation metrics.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially significant approach to a crucial problem (bias) in medical image analysis. The experimental results show promising improvements in both image fidelity and downstream task performance. However, the improvements are not overwhelmingly large, and the study is limited to a specific dataset and metadata factor. The generalizability of the approach to other datasets and metadata remains an open question. The novelty is decent, as is the significance. Therefore, while the paper makes a valuable contribution, it's not a groundbreaking advancement warranting a higher score. A score of 6.5 reflects its moderate but worthwhile impact and potential for future development.

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Do We Need Large VLMs for Spotting Soccer Actions?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17144v1)
- **Authors**: Ritabrata Chakraborty, Rajatsubhra Chakraborty, Avijit Dasgupta, Sandeep Chaurasia
- **Abstract**: Traditional video-based tasks like soccer action spotting rely heavily on visual inputs, often requiring complex and computationally expensive models to process dense video data. In this work, we propose a shift from this video-centric approach to a text-based task, making it lightweight and scalable by utilizing Large Language Models (LLMs) instead of Vision-Language Models (VLMs). We posit that expert commentary, which provides rich, fine-grained descriptions and contextual cues such as excitement and tactical insights, contains enough information to reliably spot key actions in a match. To demonstrate this, we use the SoccerNet Echoes dataset, which provides timestamped commentary, and employ a system of three LLMs acting as judges specializing in outcome, excitement, and tactics. Each LLM evaluates sliding windows of commentary to identify actions like goals, cards, and substitutions, generating accurate timestamps for these events. Our experiments show that this language-centric approach performs effectively in detecting critical match events, providing a lightweight and training-free alternative to traditional video-based methods for action spotting.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the feasibility of using Large Language Models (LLMs) and expert commentary to spot soccer actions, proposing a shift from traditional video-based approaches. It hypothesizes that rich, fine-grained commentary contains enough information to identify key events like goals, cards, and substitutions, thus enabling a lightweight and scalable text-based action spotting system. The authors utilize the SoccerNet Echoes dataset, which provides timestamped commentary, and implement a system of three specialized LLMs (outcome, excitement, and tactics judges) to evaluate sliding windows of commentary and generate accurate event timestamps. The experiments demonstrate that this language-centric approach can effectively detect critical match events, offering a training-free alternative to computationally intensive video-based methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of replacing visual input with expert commentary and using LLMs for action spotting in sports videos has some novelty. While prior work explored integrating commentary with visual information, the exclusive reliance on text and the specific use of a system with multiple specialized LLM judges appears to be a relatively novel approach. The idea of "text-based action spotting" as an alternative and potentially lightweight method is interesting.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential to reduce computational costs associated with video analysis. Traditional video-based action spotting systems can be computationally expensive, requiring extensive processing of high-resolution video data. The proposed text-based approach offers a potential alternative that is less resource-intensive and more easily scalable, especially in situations where visual data is limited or of poor quality. Furthermore, a demonstration that high quality commentary can effectively replace video opens the door to broader exploration of text as a primary modality for video understanding tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Research Question:** The paper clearly defines the research question and motivations.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The methodology is well-defined, including the use of a specific dataset, the architecture of the LLM-based system, and the evaluation metrics.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:**  The use of the SoccerNet Echoes dataset provides a robust testbed for the proposed system.
    *   **Comparative Results:** The results are presented in comparison to existing baselines, demonstrating the potential of the approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on Commentary Quality:** The approach heavily relies on the quality and availability of expert commentary. It's essential to thoroughly examine the impact of varied commentary styles, bias, and accuracy on the system's performance. What is the performance impact if the commentator is highly biased? How would you handle a game with no commentators, or very sparse or uninformative commentary? The paper mentions this in the limitations, but doesn't thoroughly address it during the analysis.
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluated Actions:** The paper primarily focuses on high-level actions like goals, cards, and substitutions. The effectiveness of the proposed approach for more granular or subtle actions, such as tactical plays or player interactions, remains unclear.
    *   **Lack of in-depth error analysis:** While the paper provides quantitative results, there could be a more detailed analysis of errors made by the LLM-based system. Where did it fail? Were those edge-cases? Were there common errors?
    *   **Limited Comparison to State-of-the-Art:** The comparison to ASTRA highlights competitiveness, but more in-depth benchmarking against a broader range of state-of-the-art action spotting models (especially VLM methods) would strengthen the analysis.
    *   **Potential for Overfitting:**  The LLMs could potentially be "overfitting" to specific commentary styles or phrases in the SoccerNet Echoes dataset. More rigorous testing on unseen datasets is needed.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the direction of action spotting research by highlighting the potential of using LLMs and text-based analysis as a complement or alternative to traditional video-based methods. It opens doors for future research into developing more sophisticated LLM-based systems for video understanding tasks.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents an innovative approach with promising results. It challenges the traditional reliance on visual input in action spotting and offers a potentially lightweight and scalable alternative. However, several limitations remain, including the dependence on commentary quality, the limited scope of evaluated actions, and the need for more comprehensive benchmarking against state-of-the-art methods. While innovative, more extensive research and analysis are necessary to fully realize the potential of the proposed approach. The paper is not groundbreaking, but a step in the right direction.

### The MedPerturb Dataset: What Non-Content Perturbations Reveal About Human and Clinical LLM Decision Making
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.17163v1)
- **Authors**: Abinitha Gourabathina, Yuexing Hao, Walter Gerych, Marzyeh Ghassemi
- **Abstract**: Clinical robustness is critical to the safe deployment of medical Large Language Models (LLMs), but key questions remain about how LLMs and humans may differ in response to the real-world variability typified by clinical settings. To address this, we introduce MedPerturb, a dataset designed to systematically evaluate medical LLMs under controlled perturbations of clinical input. MedPerturb consists of clinical vignettes spanning a range of pathologies, each transformed along three axes: (1) gender modifications (e.g., gender-swapping or gender-removal); (2) style variation (e.g., uncertain phrasing or colloquial tone); and (3) format changes (e.g., LLM-generated multi-turn conversations or summaries). With MedPerturb, we release a dataset of 800 clinical contexts grounded in realistic input variability, outputs from four LLMs, and three human expert reads per clinical context. We use MedPerturb in two case studies to reveal how shifts in gender identity cues, language style, or format reflect diverging treatment selections between humans and LLMs. We find that LLMs are more sensitive to gender and style perturbations while human annotators are more sensitive to LLM-generated format perturbations such as clinical summaries. Our results highlight the need for evaluation frameworks that go beyond static benchmarks to assess the similarity between human clinician and LLM decisions under the variability characteristic of clinical settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MedPerturb, a novel dataset designed to evaluate the robustness of medical Large Language Models (LLMs) and human clinicians to non-content perturbations in clinical text.  The dataset consists of 800 clinical vignettes, each perturbed along three axes: gender (swapping/removal), style (uncertain/colorful language), and format (multi-turn AI conversations/summaries).  The paper compares LLM and human performance (triage decisions – manage at home, visit clinic, resource allocation) on these perturbed vignettes, revealing key differences in their sensitivity to these variations. The study finds that LLMs are more sensitive to gender and style perturbations than humans, while humans are more affected by LLM-generated format changes. The study suggests the importance of evaluating LLMs beyond static benchmarks, focusing on real-world variability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the creation of the MedPerturb dataset. Perturbing clinical text along dimensions like gender, style, and format is a valuable approach to stress-testing LLMs in a realistic context. The focus on divergences between LLMs and humans, rather than solely on accuracy against a "ground truth," is a significant strength. While there's existing work on bias in LLMs and the use of LLMs in medical settings, this paper combines these elements in a way that provides direct, comparative insights.

* **Significance:** The findings have important implications for the safe and effective deployment of LLMs in healthcare.  The identified sensitivities of LLMs to gender and style, which should ideally be irrelevant to treatment decisions, raise concerns about fairness and potential bias amplification in real-world settings. The observation that humans are more influenced by LLM-generated formats (summaries/conversations) suggests a need for careful consideration of how AI systems are presented to clinicians. The results prompt a deeper inquiry into how clinicians interact with and rely on LLM tools in realistic scenarios.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-designed Dataset:** The controlled perturbation approach allows for systematic investigation of specific factors.
    * **Human Comparison:** Direct comparison to human clinician responses is crucial for understanding the potential impact of LLM integration.
    * **Real-world Relevance:**  Focus on triage decisions (Manage, Visit, Resource) directly addresses practical use cases.
    * **Comprehensive Analysis:**  The paper employs a variety of analytical techniques (ATR, Mutual Information, agreement metrics) to provide a nuanced picture.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly organized, making the methodology and findings accessible.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Human Annotator Pool:** The use of medical students as annotators introduces a potential bias and limits the generalizability to experienced clinicians. Ideally, this could be supplemented with expert attending physician reads.
    * **LLM-Generated Perturbations:** The use of LLMs to create stylistic perturbations introduces the possibility of unintended artifacts or biases in the generated text, affecting the validity of the study.
    * **Specific Triage Questions:**  The reliance on a specific set of triage questions may limit the findings' applicability to other clinical decision-making tasks.
    * **Generalizability to different specialties:** The relative contributions from different specialties/clinical content in the MedQA-USMLE could be analyzed.

* **Potential Influence:** MedPerturb provides a valuable resource for future research in medical AI safety and fairness. The paper's findings can inform the design of more robust and reliable LLMs for healthcare and guide the development of evaluation frameworks that go beyond static accuracy metrics.  The paper may stimulate work into creating better, less biased clinical data sources.

**Overall Score:** 7

**Rationale:** The paper makes a solid contribution through the creation of MedPerturb and the rigorous analysis of LLM vs. human decision-making under controlled perturbations. While there are limitations related to the annotator pool and reliance on LLM-generated perturbations, the findings are significant and have potential for substantial impact on the field of medical AI. Further work will be needed to address the identified weaknesses and expand the scope of the study, but the paper provides a valuable foundation for future research.
